Transcript

0:02 · COMMISSIONER GREGG BISHOP: The “urban heat island” effect affects a lot of cities like New York. Because a tremendous amount of buildings, a lot of concrete, and little vegetation increases the overall temperature of the city. And when the city is really, really, really hot, it affects the health of our citizens, individuals who may suffer from asthma, or may be susceptible to heat waves, our elderly population. So, heat actually affects life and safety.

0:31 · One of the strategies that we have to combat the urban heat island effect is the NYC CoolRoofs initiative, which works simply by painting the roofs white.

0:39 · BRIDGET CARTER: See how the coating is going inside the seams?

0:42 · Put it on as heavy as you can get it.

0:44 · BISHOP: Many buildings, because of the black roof, absorb heat. And painting them white reduces the internal temperature of a building by 10-30 percent.

0:53 · CARTER: Remember guys, don’t stretch the coating.

0:55 · Don’t stretch the coating.

0:56 · Where is your bucket?

0:57 · That reflective coating is going to keep the building cooler, conserve energy for the people that live in the building— less air conditioning and less fans being run. People can save some money.

1:07 · Very energy efficient.

1:08 · BISHOP: This CoolRoofs program is really a part of the Mayor’s strategy to lower New York City’s emissions by 80 percent by 2050, and to really help New York City become a great partner in fighting climate change.

Extreme Heat Is the New Normal

New York Times Opinion, 08 28, 2026

Video (15:42)
Description

We filmed “A New Inferno,” the short documentary above, in Phoenix, one of the hottest cities in America, through some of the city’s hottest days of 2025, during one of the hottest years on record globally. On one of our production days, temperatures reached 118 while we were filming outside.

In the face of extreme heat, we documented firefighters and paramedics of the Phoenix Fire Department saving lives in real time. We wanted to call attention to a specialized technique of ice bag immersion for people facing life-threatening heat stroke. We hope that telling their story and showcasing the efficacy of this strategy will encourage wider adoption of this protocol across the country.

Yet this procedure is ultimately a stopgap, as firefighters do their best to adapt to the climate crisis in front of them. The underlying story of heat in Phoenix is one of broader, interlocking crises: homelessness and substance use disorders that are deeply entangled with urban heat, each amplifying the other, illustrating how climate change affects the disenfranchised most brutally.

With our film, we aim to reframe how the public thinks about heat: as an invisible, silent killer reshaping the rhythms of urban life.

Documentary and text by By Nita Blum-Reddick and Jonathan Pickett

More from Op-Docs: https://www.nytimes.com/column/op-docs

Housing Segregation and Redlining in America A Short History Code Switch NPR

NPR, 04 11, 2018

Video (6:36)
Description

In 1968, Congress passed the Fair Housing Act that made it illegal to discriminate in housing. Gene Demby of NPR’s Code Switch explains why neighborhoods are still so segregated today.

• Read “How Segregation Shapes Fatal Police Violence” at https://n.pr/2Ic6A1Q

• Read or listen to: “‘The Color Of Law’ Details How U.S. Housing Policies Created Segregation” at https://n.pr/2HgqATh

WHAT’S CODE SWITCH?

It’s the fearless conversations about race that you’ve been waiting for! Hosted by journalists of color, our podcast tackles the subject of race head-on. We explore how it impacts every part of society — from politics and pop culture to history, sports and everything in between. This podcast makes ALL OF US part of the conversation — because we’re all part of the story.

0:00 · Chris Rock: You know what’s so sad, man?

0:00 · You know what’s wild?

0:01 · Martin Luther King stood for nonviolence.

0:13 · Gene: That, of course, is Chris Rock’s famous joke about streets named for Martin Luther King Jr., which tend to be in – let’s say distressed areas.

0:22 · And he’s not wrong, because if you look at the way housing segregation works in America you can see how things ended up this way.

0:28 · Once you see it, you won’t be able to unsee it.

0:35 · I want to show you how to see housing segregation in schools, in health, in family wealth, in policing.

0:41 · But first, an explanatory comma.

0:45 · It’s the 1930s in the wake of the Great Depression.

0:47 · FDR is president.

0:48 · He wants to bring economic relief to millions of Americans through a collection of federal programs and projects called The New Deal.

0:54 · One part of that “new deal” was The National Housing Act of 1934, which introduced ideas like the 30-year mortgage and low, fixed interest rates.

1:03 · So now you have all these lower-income people who can afford homes, but how do you make sure they don’t default on their new mortgages?

1:09 · Enter the Home Owners Loan Corp.

1:12 · The HOLC created residential security maps.

RESIDENTIAL SECURITY MAPS

1:15 · And these maps? They’re where the term redlining comes from.

1:20 · Green meant “best area, best people,” aka businessmen; blue meant “good people,” like white-collar families; yellow meant a “declining area,” with working class families; and red meant “detrimental influences, hazardous,” like “foreign-born” people, “low-class whites,” and – most significantly – “Negroes.”

1:41 · Again and again on these HOLC maps, one of the most consistent criteria for redlined neighborhoods is the presence of black and

brown people.

1:49 · Let’s be clear.

1:50 · Studies show that people who lived in redlined areas were not necessarily more likely to default on their mortgages.

1:57 · But redlining made it difficult — if not impossible — to buy or refinance.

2:02 · So landlords abandon their properties.

2:04 · City services become unreliable.

2:06 · In most places, crime increases.

2:09 · And property values drop.

2:11 · All of these conditions fester for 30 years as white people flee to the brand new suburbs popping up all over the country.

2:18 · Many of those suburbs institute rules, called covenants, that explicitly forbid selling homes to black people.

2:23 · And all of this was perfectly legal.

2:27 · Now it’s 1968.

2:29 · And MLK is assassinated.

2:31 · News Report: Good evening.

2:32 · The Rev. Dr. Martin Luther King, 39 years old, The apostle of nonviolence in the civil rights movement has been shot to death in Memphis, Tenn.

2:39 · Martin Luther King was shot and was killed tonight in Memphis, Tenn.

2:42 · In the aftermath, Congress passes the Fair Housing Act of 1968.

2:47 · It’s a policy meant to encourage equal housing opportunities regardless of race, or religion or national origin.

2:52 · And it offers protections for future homeowners and renters, but does little to fix the damage already done.

2:58 · Over the next 50 years, the Fair Housing Act is rarely enforced.

3:02 · So you can still see housing segregation and its effects, in Baltimore and often along any MLK Boulevard in any U.S. city.

3:11 · Like its effects on wealth.

WEALTH

3:13 · So homeownership is the major way Americans create wealth, right?

3:16 · Well, discrimination in housing is the major reason that black families up and down the income scale have a tiny fraction of the family wealth that white families do – even white families with less education and lower incomes.

3:28 · For almost 30 years, 98 percent of FHA loans were handed out to white borrowers.

3:33 · Not only were black neighborhoods redlined, and not only was the Fair Housing Act selectively enforced, if at all, but it is still today much harder for a black person to get a mortgage or home loan than it is for a white person.

3:46 · John: Families are fearful of speaking up about a basic human right that should be afforded to everyone in the world but definitely in the richest country in the world.

3:59 · And housing segregation in schools.

4:02 · The primary way that Americans pay for public schools is by paying property taxes.

4:07 · People who live in more valuable homes have better-funded local schools, better-paid teachers, better school facilities and more resources.

4:15 · Here’s a feedback loop: The better the schools in a neighborhood, the more those homes in that neighborhood are worth.

4:21 · And the higher the property values of those homes, the more money there is for schools.

4:25 · And so on and so on.

4:27 · And housing segregation in health.

4:29 · Because of urban planning that benefited those richer, whiter neighborhoods, people of color are more likely to live near industrial plants that spew toxic fumes; they’re more likely to live far away from grocery stores with fresh food, and in places where

the water isn’t drinkable.

HEALTH

4:43 · They’re more likely to live in neighborhoods with crumbling infrastructure, and in homes with toxic paint.

4:49 · Karen: When you’re living with rats, roaches, and things like that – that’s deplorable.

4:53 · You cannot have that kind of stuff with children running around in the building.

4:56 · A building that may be full of lead.

5:00 · And, not coincidentally, people of color have higher incidences of certain cancers, asthma and heart disease.

5:08 · And housing segregation in policing.

POLICING

5:11 · Housing segregation means we are having vastly different experiences with crime and vastly different experiences with policing.

5:17 · Because our neighborhoods are so segregated, sometimes racial profiling can be camouflaged as spatial profiling — where living in certain areas can make you more likely to be stopped by the police.

5:29 · And it means people have a lot of unnecessary contact with the criminal justice system just because of where they live.

5:38 · Reggie: The problem in our city?

5:41 · The police and the citizens are fighting.

5:43 · They keep targeting my brothers and sisters who don’t really have nothing.

5:49 · And that heavy, aggressive kind of policing that you see in black neighborhoods in particular makes people feel like they can’t trust the police.

5:55 · And when people don’t trust the police, crimes go unsolved and people have to find other ways to keep themselves safe.

6:03 · But, of course, it’s not just Baltimore.

6:05 · Because housing segregation and discrimination fundamentally shape the lives of people in nearly every major American city.

6:14 · It really is in everything.

How America’s hottest city is trying to cool down

Vox, 09 20, 2021

Video (6:48)
Description

Can trees help save Phoenix from extreme heat?

Subscribe and turn on notifications 🔔 so you don’t miss any videos: http://goo.gl/0bsAjO

It’s time to stop looking at trees as a form of “beautification.” They are, instead, a living form of infrastructure, providing a variety of services that include stormwater management, air filtering, carbon sequestration, and, most importantly for a city like Phoenix, Arizona, they cool the environment around them.

Trees can lower neighborhood temperatures in three ways: 1) Their shade prevents solar radiation from hitting paved surfaces like concrete and asphalt, which absorb energy and rerelease it into the air as heat. 2) Their leaves pull heat from the immediate area in order to evapotranspirate water that’s drawn from the soil. And, 3) If you’re standing under one, a tree protects your body directly from the sun’s rays. If you’ve ever made a summer visit to a dry, hot city like Phoenix, you’ll know how important shade is for making any outdoor experiences tolerable.

As Phoenix deals with a rising frequency of extreme heat waves — which aren’t only deadly, but also cause worrisome spikes in energy demand — the city is looking to trees as part of its heat mitigation strategy. Phoenix isn’t devoid of trees, but they’re distributed unevenly across the city. A quick glance at a satellite image of the metro area reveals substantial green splotches in the north and east and brown ones in the south and west, where many lower-income neighborhoods are located.

So Phoenix recently pledged to reach “tree equity” by 2030, under an agreement with American Forests, a national tree organization. I visited Phoenix recently to take a look at the current state of the city’s urban forest. In this video, we use drone imagery and thermal cameras to understand how the urban design of the city contributes to extreme heat, and what it can do to cool down.

This is the first video of five videos we’re releasing on climate change. You can watch the second video, about high-voltage transmission lines ⚡️ and why the US isn’t ready for clean energy, here: https://youtu.be/s3ScJ_FwaZk

And the third video dives into prescribed burns, and how a decade of suppressing forest fires 🔥 may have made them worse, here: https://youtu.be/0o6ezu_h6iE

How Do You Share a Shrinking River Fairly

Transcript
The Imperial Valley oasis

0:01 · The Imperial Valley sits downstream of two massive reservoirs: Lake Powell and Lake Mead.

0:08 · Together, they act as a giant savings account, buffering water users from the whims of weather and the uncertainty of the annual snowpack.

0:18 · The incongruous, almost magical, nature of this lush oasis in the desert really dawned on me during a visit to Gina Dockstader’s Citrus Grove.

0:29 · DOCKSTADER: What do you think, Lalo, you want to pick one off for us?

0:32 · This one right here? DOCKSTADER: Yeah.

0:33 · O’BRIEN: There we go.

0:34 · (voiceover): It was January, early morning, not long after sunrise, when she gave me a fresh-as-it-gets taste test.

0:48 · All right, so– and so, what’s the proper way to eat it?

0:53 · the juice is great. O’BRIEN: Oh, my gosh.

0:54 · Oh, that’s delicious.

0:59 · Vegetables are.

1:01 · About two-thirds of the winter produce sold in the U.S. is grown here.

1:07 · Fine-looking lemons here!

1:21 · Right.

1:22 · High economic value in a small area.

Water use and crop priorities

1:31 · I am fourth-generation, and my son is fifth-generation.

1:34 · We are very, very proud of that fact.

1:36 · O’BRIEN (voiceover): Gina is also a director on the board of the Imperial Irrigation District– the public agency that holds and manages the single largest entitlement to water from the Colorado River.

1:48 · Besides citrus, her family grows dates and, like just about everyone here, alfalfa and Bermuda grass, both of which get bundled up into hay bales to be fed to livestock.

2:02 · You can’t put all your eggs in one basket, right?

2:04 · Otherwise, how do you make it from year to year?

2:05 · O’BRIEN: No crop demands more Colorado River water than alfalfa.

2:10 · Farmers across the basin use roughly five to six million acre-feet a year to grow hay for cattle and dairy operations.

2:19 · The Imperial Valley is one of the world’s most productive hay-growing regions.

2:23 · Thanks to a year-round growing season and easy access to Pacific ports, as much as half of its alfalfa is exported to Asia and the Middle East– controversial destinations for a crop irrigated by one of America’s most stressed rivers.

2:43 · You’re kind of shipping the Colorado River to other countries. DOCKSTADER: Right.

3:04 · Because they’re feeding their cattle, and that’s how you’re getting leather products back into the United States.

3:11 · O’BRIEN: Alfalfa is a thirsty crop, and the fact that a significant share of it ultimately leaves the country doesn’t sit well with many people.

3:21 · But Gina says folks in the Imperial Valley are accustomed to being in the crosshairs.

3:27 · DOCKSTADER: We’ve always felt that other people were looking at our water because we have such a significant… priority right.

Water management and policy

3:40 · O’BRIEN: That’s exactly what happened in 2003.

3:43 · As it became clear the Colorado River could no longer meet all the demands placed on it, the federal government required California to live within its legal allocation.

3:55 · The result was the Quantification Settlement Agreement, or QSA.

4:00 · It requires the Imperial Irrigation District to transfer water to San Diego.

4:06 · In return, the San Diego County Water Authority helps fund conservation projects that enable farmers to use less water while keeping much of their land in production.

4:18 · DOCKSTADER: Now I can pay for a sprinkler pipe.

4:20 · I can sprinkle, I can use less water to grow the same crop, and that’s how the water gets created.

4:27 · We’re getting smarter.

4:28 · Farmers are resilient.

4:30 · They, we all know we have to do more with less.

4:33 · ♪ O’BRIEN (voiceover): That message is resonating in the Imperial Valley.

4:55 · feet going into the field.

Modern irrigation strategies

5:21 · So, how long will this gate be open today?

6:05 · ♪ O’BRIEN: Flood irrigation requires very little energy.

6:10 · But because it wets the entire soil surface, more water is lost to evaporation in hot, dry climates than with sprinkler or drip irrigation.

Adapting to a limited supply

7:04 · Farmers are paid to cease irrigating some of their crops during the hot summer months when evaporation is at its peak.

7:55 · The century-old system that created this agricultural oasis is built on the notion of an endless supply of cheap water.

8:04 · But everything has its limits.

8:08 · It’s hard to do this with less water is what it comes down to. - Exactly, yeah, and we’re all on a budget. We’re all on a water budget.

How redlining prevented Black and Brown families from becoming home owners

Harvard Kennedy School, 09 19, 2023

Video (4:28)
Description

The term “redlining” refers to the practice by banks of excluding Black and Brown Americans from receiving loans to buy homes and make improvements to their communities. Redlining arose during the Great Depression, when the federal government sought to boost homeownership by encouraging banks to lend to more Americans. Many neighborhoods, specifically those with majority Black and Brown residents, were deemed undeserving of these loans. Though redlining was made illegal by the Fair Housing Act, the effects of this practice still reverberate to this day.

In this video, Khalil Gibran Muhammad, Ford Foundation Professor of History, Race, and Public Policy at Harvard Kennedy School and director of the Institutional Antiracism and Accountability Project, provides background on the history and legacy of redlining.

About Harvard Kennedy School:
The John F. Kennedy School of Government at Harvard University is a graduate and professional school that brings together students, scholars, and practitioners who combine thought and action to make the world a better place.
Our mission is to improve public policy and public leadership across the United States and around the world so that people can lead safer, freer, and more prosperous lives. Harvard Kennedy School teaches current and future leaders the skills they need to effectively advance the public purpose in the public, nonprofit, and private sectors. Our renowned faculty and trailblazing research centers pioneer bold new ideas. And as the most international school at Harvard, we convene global leaders in the Forum, host visiting experts in the classroom, and attract a diverse community of faculty, students, and staff.
##### Transcript
0:00 · In every way imaginable, the consequences of redlining to many black and brown communities across America meant that those communities had the least and the last of everything.
0:16 · So I want to talk about redlining, because this is a term you may have heard, but most people don’t actually know the history.
0:24 · In 1933, in the midst of America’s Great Depression, when unemployment rates were 25% and millions of people were not only out of work, but many of them were houseless, the federal government under President Franklin Delano Roosevelt established an agency called the Home Owners Loan Corporation.
0:42 · Basically, it tried to make home buying much more affordable by subsidizing the cost of building and lending such that people would be able to afford to buy for the first time.
0:55 · Millions of Americans participated, except that when the Home Owners Loan Corporation began to issue mortgages, it came up with a scheme to decide which communities were deserving of loans and which ones weren’t.
1:10 · They made maps and used a color coded scheme.
1:14 · Green meant this was a community that you could lend to and red you could not.
1:19 · The basic decision they made was to determine that any community with the presence of black people or brown people or people who, perhaps, spoke Italian, in certain parts of the country could not receive support from the Home Owners Loan Corporation.
1:36 · This created a system that came to be known as redlining because communities that were deemed risky by the mere presence of people of color, most especially black people, were disqualified from getting direct support from the federal government to purchase homes.
1:53 · This policy lasted until 1968, when the federal government passed the Fair Housing Act, which made all forms of discrimination in the buying and selling or renting of homes illegal.
2:08 · But the truth is that the legacies of redlining from that moment to the present meant that black people were subjected to other forms of exploitative lending.
2:18 · A system called contract buying grew up as a way to sell black people homes on a layaway plan.
2:26 · You made set payments over an extended period of time, but you never owned the home until the very last payment.
2:34 · It was very common for unscrupulous sellers to withhold that final payment in an effort to take back the home from a person who had worked incredibly hard to buy it.
2:45 · Home ownership has been the most important asset that a typical white family has in order to do simple things like borrow money to send a child to college.
2:58 · But most importantly, they have financial freedom.
3:01 · But for communities that have been subjected to redlining, they didn’t have those advantages.
3:08 · Not only that, the very communities that they lived in suffered from divestment, including the lack of grocery stores, the lack of businesses, the imposition of federal highways that often bisected their communities.
3:24 · In every way imaginable, the consequences of redlining to many black and brown communities across America meant that those communities had the least and the last of everything.
3:38 · And to this day, the median wages and income of those communities, and the financial insecurity that people who live in redlined communities face, has long term health consequences for families, has educational consequences for young people whose schools often don’t have the tax basis to give them the same resources that communities that were never redlined actually have.
#### How Singapore solved its looming water crisis
DW Planet A, 09 20, 2024
##### Video (12:46)
##### Description
In Singapore, water supply impacted wars, the economy and people’s health. Now, one of the most water-stressed countries is teaching the world how to handle water when there is hardly any.

0:20 · This is how Singapore is transforming itself from one of the most water-stressed countries in the world to self-sufficiency, from water rags – to riches.
###### The challenge
0:36 · Welcome to Singapore!
0:37 · A vibrant multicultural city-state, right on the equator.

0:55 · There’s just that one thing…
0:56 · Singapore has no natural freshwater resources.
1:00 · And the way its economy and population are growing, total water demand could almost double by 2060.

1:13 · That’s Peter Gleick, he’s an award-winning water expert.
1:16 · He puts Singapore’s success down to what he calls the “soft path” approach to their problems.
1:21 · “So the soft path for water is in contrast to what we have done over the last century and more, more than a century, the hard path.
1:29 · To build hard infrastructure, to take more and more water out of nature, to ignore the consequences of our water policies for nature, to build narrow institutions.
1:39 · And the soft path tries to turn that around and say, let’s use water efficiently and carefully.
1:45 · Let’s stop wasting water.
1:47 · Let’s look at new sources of supply.”
1:50 · To understand how Singapore can pull this off, you first need to understand where they’re coming from.
###### History
1:58 · “The Singapore water story starts with World War 2.”
2:02 · That’s Jon Church, the guy for water at the United Nations.
2:06 · Singaporeans would argue that the story goes back a lot longer, but 1942 is certainly when the issue grabbed the world’s attention.
2:13 · That’s when allied troops, namely British, Australian and Indian forces, were battling fascist Japan on Singapore Island, which at the time was a British colony.
2:24 · The allies depended on imported water which was a bit of a problem when Japanese forces blew up the pipes transporting that water.
2:30 · “The bridge that connects Malaysia and Singapore was bombed.
2:35 · The result was lack of water in the city, a terrible episode of water scarcity.”
2:43 · The Allies lost the battle of Singapore, but after Japan’s overall surrender, the island remained in British hands.
2:50 · It took more than 20 years until it became fully independent.
2:53 · But political liberation didn’t free Singapore from its water crisis: water rationing, catastrophic sanitation, and regular floods continued.
3:01 · With its fate now in its own hands, Singapore began planning for the long-term.
3:06 · Cecilia Tortajada has spent almost three decades researching water policy and innovations around the world and is fascinated by Singapore’s path.
3:15 · “When Singapore became independent, they started planning to be water independent, as well as food independent, energy independent, but to have systems that can sustain stress.
3:28 · That was in 1965 for 2060.”
###### Step 1: Imported Water
3:32 · Their initial masterplan was fine-tuned over the years into a water strategy which they call their “4 national taps”.
3:40 · First: Water imports Second: Desalination Third: Local Catchment And fourth, something they call “NEWater”.
3:50 · Let’s dive into it!
3:53 · Firstly, Singapore knew there was plenty of water, right here: Two deals to import water from Malaysia in the 60’s, got the taps running again.
4:03 · It’s cheap and millions of liters are pumped over the border every day.
4:07 · But buying half your water from your neighbor isn’t sustainable long-term.
4:12 · On the contrary: From the start, Malaysia made threats to cut off the supply and argued over its price.
4:18 · The tensions have even led to warnings of a military conflict.
4:21 · Singapore’s leaders know their weak spot, so they want to stop importing water by 2061.
4:27 · So, it’s all the more important that the other three taps become more than a drop in the ocean.
4:31 · “Water planning is very important because they have so little water, Singapore must make sure, that this water is used wisely.
4:41 · And the objective of this master plan is to make the most out of every single drop of water.”
4:48 · This means keeping rivers and drains clean, investing billions, starting to collect more water, cleaning it and use what the island state Singapore already has.
###### Step 2: Desalination

6:04 · But that’s no way near self-sufficiency.

###### Step 3: Local Catchment
6:09 · Bingo! Rain!
6:11 · A lot of it, and Singapore is a master of maxing that out.
6:15 · Two third of the entire state’s surface is used for rainwater catchment.

6:43 · And treated for further use.

6:51 · The biggest is Marina Barrage, with an area of 10,000 hectares.

7:08 · Even this water could potentially be treated and reused.
7:11 · By 2060, the government wants to use 90% of the land area for rain catchment.
###### Step 4: NEWater
7:18 · Catching rainwater is one thing, but what about the water already in circulation?
7:25 · “All drainage water is collected, and treated and reused as much as possible.
7:32 · Most countries, they do not invest, not even a fraction of what Singapore is investing.”
7:38 · The water authorities built a 206 kilometer sewage pipe-highway, costing 10 billion dollars that guides the city’s sewage into state-of-the-art reclamation and cleaning facilities.
7:50 · Ok, Singapore is by some measures the 5th richest country in the world so it can afford to splash out like that.
7:56 · It’s also a lot easier to push through such projects when you have the sort of “controlled democracy”, run by the same party, that Singapore has had since independence in 1965.
8:07 · Anyway, the entire city’s underground is basically one big sewer network.
8:12 · But the pride of Singapore’s water strategy is what happens after collecting the water – the treatment.
8:19 · The authorities call it NEWater.
8:21 · It’s produced by microfiltration, reverse osmosis and UV radiation.
8:29 · “Some of that water is so high quality, Singapore is using it in their chip manufacturing industry, which requires ultra-pure water, which is an indication of how good a quality of that water is.”
8:42 · Did you know, that around the world, half of our wastewater is released untreated?
8:46 · And only 11% is actually reused.
8:49 · In Singapore about 30% percent of the entire drinkable and non-drinkable demand can be met by recycling used water.
8:57 · And they want to increase that to 55% by 2060.
9:02 · Most of it is used by industry, only a fraction of it is mixed into the drinking water supply, because we’re all so squeamish.
9:09 · “It’s controversial because we’re taking wastewater which, you know, goes down our drains, we flush it down our toilets, industry dumps wastewater.
9:17 · And we typically think of that as a, a liability, as something to get rid of.”
###### Policy and Education
9:22 · So how do you get people on board?
9:25 · “Using the flush saver cistern bag and save with every flush!”
9:29 · “Make saving water pay!”
9:30 · “Reduce water consumption!”
9:32 · “Save up to 10 liters of water per hour!”
9:35 · Nationwide campaigns encourage people to install water-saving fittings.
9:38 · And nicely, if you install those fittings, you get discount vouchers for other sustainable products.
9:44 · Digital water meters track each household’s water consumption and detect leaks in pipes.
9:49 · That’s how Singapore comes down to an incredible 5% water loss from leaks.
9:53 · Which is first class.
9:55 · Globally, the average is estimated to be 30%.
9:58 · “What they’ve done incredibly well, in my opinion, is on the side of water education.
10:04 · They’ve educated their community about their water situation, about their water challenges, and about the solutions that they’ve chosen to push.”
###### Conclusion
10:14 · As good as Singapore is doing, it has big advantages over other countries.
10:18 · Besides being rich and a tightly-controlled society, the city only has a tiny agricultural sector and can focus almost entirely on urban and industrial wastewater.
10:29 · So, they have one problem less as food production pollutes and consumes enormous amounts of fresh water in many parts of the world.
10:36 · Despite that, they’ve shown: a lot is possible.
10:41 · “Everywhere in the world, when we start, again talking about water - when we see our management, how can it be improved?
10:50 · Who would be involved?
10:52 · How can we pay for it?
10:54 · There is always a “but”.
10:55 · “But” can we do it?
10:56 · And in Singapore it’s not.
10:58 · In Singapore it’s: how is it that we are going to do it?
11:01 · So, it’s a very different perspective, its proactive.
11:06 · They move very fast.
11:08 · So once a decision has been taken the things start happening.”
11:14 · So, to sum up: Incentivizing low-cost water-saving fittings could be easy for every government to implement.
11:20 · Desalination is only an option for coastal regions and it requires a lot of energy, often dirty fossil fuels.
11:27 · Singapore’s catchment, treatment and purification facilities are world class, as the result of a lot of investment in innovation and research.
11:34 · “But it turns out that a dollar invested in an efficiency improvement is much more productive than a dollar invested in new supply.”
11:45 · Reusing wastewater seems a no-brainer.
11:47 · It’s safe and tested to work and the tech is available.
11:52 · “This is really important to find the best solutions to every country’s problems, but also long-term planning.
12:01 · Singapore, from all the way from independence has been planning its way through water security.
12:09 · In Singapore it’s extremely high on the political agenda, all the way up to the Prime Minister.
12:16 · It’s something which is mainstreamed in each and every policy adopted and implemented by the country.”
12:25 · Singapore is blazing a trail in tackling some of the world’s biggest water challenges in the face of climate change.
12:30 · It’s not just about money, but also about decision-making, policy and long-term planning.
12:35 · Some of the ingredients with which any region could make it from water rags to riches.
#### How systemic racism shaped the ecosystems of U.S. cities

Historically Black and immigrant communities are more vulnerable to environmental stressors

##### Transcript
0:01 · The urban environment is a feat of human decision-making— from where to develop street grids lined with large glass office buildings, to which green spaces to preserve for outdoor recreation.
0:13 · How humans make these choices, and the natural environment’s response, can create extremely different ecosystems in neighborhoods just a few blocks away from each other.
0:23 · Researchers delved into dozens of studies to understand what drives these variations.
0:29 · One well documented phenomenon in cities is the relationship between wealth and biodiversity.
0:35 · More affluent areas generally have greater canopy cover, with larger, mature trees, as well as a higher diversity of birds.
0:45 · This is known as the “luxury effect.”
0:48 · Low-income areas typically have more man-made surfaces— including major heat-absorbers like asphalt and concrete, compared to higher-income areas.
0:59 · The lack of shade from trees and vegetation, combined with more asphalt and concrete, makes it hotter in these neighborhoods— a phenomenon known as an “urban heat island.”
1:10 · These communities are also more likely to be near sources of air pollutants, like expansive interstates pumping car exhaust.
1:19 · Although wealth can sometimes predict the outcomes of an urban ecosystem, there isn’t always a clear relationship.
1:26 · In some cases, the racial make-up of a neighborhood is better than income when forecasting canopy cover.
1:33 · Researchers explored studies that highlight how systemic racism shaped our build environment.
1:39 · One glaring example in the United States is residential segregation.
1:44 · From 1933-1968, the federal government limited access to homeownership among Black Americans through a practice known as redlining.
1:55 · Neighborhoods were graded based on the riskiness of providing a mortgage in the area.
2:00 · The grades ranged from A, the most desirable areas, full of wealthy businessmen, to D, neighborhoods considered high-risk investments due to a combination of factors: from their environmental quality and distance from large industrial factories to the main determinant, the presence of foreign-born and Black people.
2:22 · The inability for Black people to buy homes in these neighborhoods meant many types of capital investments left— landlords abandoned their properties, and public services, such as transportation and park maintenance, weren’t prioritized.
2:36 · Today, many U.S. cities still exhibit long-lasting environmental impacts from this discrimination.
2:42 · Neighborhoods which had a “D” grade in the 60s have about half the canopy cover of historically white communities.
2:50 · Some studies evaluate how the current environment relates to historically redlined communities, but researchers did not find any research that traces how ecosystems changed over time because of this segregation.
3:03 · They propose that because habitable regions are more spread out in these areas, animal populations struggle to breed since they are cut off from each other.
3:14 · The reduced breeding options for animals could have decreased gene diversity, an important way for animals to adapt to changing environments.
3:23 · And decreased gene flow of native species could promote the abundance of pest species, like mosquitos and brown rats, which carry diseases that can infect humans.
3:34 · The combination of all these outcomes— from urban heat islands to increased pests— make low-income, marginalized communities far more vulnerable to risks like climate change.
3:47 · Scientists say such evidence shows that conservation and social justice cross paths— especially along neighborhood streets.

#### How to Fix New York City’s $500M Trash Problem WSJ Pro Perfected
The Wall Street Journal, 08 14, 2026
##### Video (6:52)
##### Description
New York City handles nearly 3 million tons of trash a year. And much of it ends up on the curb in loose plastic bags — before getting shipped to landfills outside of the city altogether, on barges and then trains. This system costs the city around $500 million a year. So, can the largest city in the United States modernize its waste management?
WSJ explores how experts and city officials plan to tackle New York City’s giant trash problem.

##### Transcript
###### NYC’s trash problem
0:00 · Every year, New York City handles nearly three million tons of trash. For decades, mountains of garbage bags have lined the streets, feeding rats and blocking sidewalks.
0:10 · Most people think that garbage goes up to solid waste heaven. It does quite the opposite.
0:15 · Now, the Sanitation Department says it has a solution. Its trash revolution program aims to finally put a lid on the countless garbage bags piling city blocks.
0:24 · But can redesigning the way New Yorkers throw out their trash solve a problem decades in the making?
0:29 · We asked people who know the system best. Steven Cohen is the Director of the Sustainability Management Program at Columbia University, and he says one of New York’s biggest solutions is surprisingly simple, changing how the city stores and collects its garbage. Instead of leaving flimsy trash bags on the sidewalk, the city’s sanitation department is now moving towards sealed containers emptied by mechanized trucks.
###### Possible solutions
0:55 · The motto of the Sanitation Department is New York’s strongest. And now I think we’re going to call them New York’s smartest because they figured out a way to get the machine to do the work.
1:04 · But changing how New York handles its garbage means undoing a system that’s been in place for more than half a century.
###### Why the issue exists
1:10 · We used to have metal cans that everybody had to put their garbage in that you would have to wash out after use. People in apartment buildings, superintendents didn’t like doing that so much.
1:21 · That’s how New Yorkers put out their trash until the late 1960s. But when sanitation workers went on strike in 1968, residents switched to plastic garbage bags. And when the strike ended, the bags stayed.
1:33 · This was really great news for the rats because it essentially created a buffet for them all over New York City of free food, because rats have no trouble at all getting into garbage bags.
1:43 · More than 50 years later, trash bags are just as common as the vermin they attract, and the city’s layout makes the problem even harder. Unlike other major American cities, Manhattan was built largely without alleys, leaving garbage with only one place to wait for pickup, the curb. The city is now trying to take those bags off the sidewalk altogether, replacing them with permanent containers like these.
###### What the sanitation department does now
2:05 · The model that’s used everywhere in the United States, either dumpsters in alleys, or 96 gallon sort of wheeled containers, wasn’t feasible in New York City, because of our density and because of the way that our city’s been built. So we looked to the model used in Europe, the stationary on-street containers, as the solution for a large number of our buildings.
2:27 · Buildings can store trash in these bins throughout the week, and they all get emptied on pickup days.
2:32 · You have a bin that’s made of plastic, that’s very durable, and there’s a lot of garbage in that bin. And you have a mechanical arm picking up a tremendous amount of garbage and dumping it into the truck. The worker you see here is operating a machine and he doesn’t have to go out and pick up the garbage himself.
2:52 · And these bins seem to be working to keep out pests.
2:55 · The pace is a little bit slower, but I think overall, I think for the community it’s great. It does, we’ve seen the difference. I know they’ve been trying to tackle the rat situation, and it’s definitely improved.
3:06 · Businesses have been required to use closed bins since March 2024. And next year, the city plans to expand these residential containers to six more districts. By 2031, officials say New York City will be fully containerized.
3:19 · This is a way to collect the garbage in a more efficient and frankly humane way, protecting the occupational health and safety of the workers. And I think this is really how robotics are changing all sorts of manual labor.
###### The bigger problem
3:34 · In Cohen’s vision for waste in the city, robotics would also play a larger role, sorting through the trash for recyclables to divert waste from landfills.
3:42 · Humans do not want to spend their day sorting garbage. Robots could care less. And what AI can do is train those robots to really be able to identify what’s coming past them. And as we get better at that, that waste stream suddenly becomes a trove of resources.
4:02 · That could address a much larger challenge for New York. The city has nowhere to put most of its garbage.
4:08 · The first place the waste goes in New York City is to a waste transfer station.
4:12 · New York once relied on its own landfills, but as they filled up, the city gradually closed them. Fresh Kills, which was the largest landfill in the world at the time, closed in 2001.
4:22 · We just ran out of space.
4:24 · Today, New York’s trash is offloaded at transfer stations, packed into shipping containers, and sent to landfills and waste-to-energy plants across the country. That process costs around $500 million per year. Waste-to-energy plants burn garbage to generate usable energy, offering an alternative to burying trash in landfills. But either way, the city still has to send its waste away.
4:45 · In the map here, what you see is that, once we could no longer landfill in the five boroughs, we had to go outside the city. And you can see we go already 1,000 miles or longer to find a place to bring our garbage. We go wherever we can.
5:05 · We’re constantly looking.
5:07 · That problem is only going to get worse.
5:10 · We are going to be forced to ship our trash further and further, to places in the south and the Midwest where there’s more space, there’s more landfills, there’s more capacity. And that’s going to become really expensive.
5:22 · Cohen’s proposal could change that equation by reducing how much waste needs to be shipped out of the city in the first place.
5:27 · The long-term vision I would have would be to build a waste processing plant that uses artificial intelligence and robotics to separate the useful resources and create separate places for those substances to be reused.
5:46 · This concept already exists.
5:48 · Materials processing facilities use automated systems to sort recyclables, but Cohen’s proposal is more ambitious, applying that technology to New York’s entire waste stream. Some tech companies are already experimenting with robots that can sort waste autonomously, separating out materials of all categories to be repurposed. In New York, Cohen says the technology would be economically transformative, using trash as a product to be sold rather than disposed of.
###### The bigger solution
6:13 · The question is how a city as large as New York can implement this technology in the long term. The Sanitation Department has multiple programs in place for recycling and composting, but the technology is still developing. So as New York’s trash disposal gets more expensive, recovering what’s inside could become increasingly valuable. But making that vision a reality requires a lot more than just rethinking the city’s trash collection.
6:36 · Anything that isn’t grown, anything that isn’t organic is finite. And at a certain point, digging it out of the planet becomes more expensive than digging it out of your garbage.

#### How urban heat islands make the impacts of excessive heat worse
PBS NewsHour, 07 27, 2023
##### Video (6:11)
##### Description
Living in certain parts of a city can make the impacts of extreme heat worse. That’s because of a phenomenon called the urban heat island effect. A recent report by the research group Climate Central showed that more than 40 million Americans live in these hot spots. William Brangham discussed what this means for those residents with Michael Mendez of the University of California, Irvine.

###### What is an urban heat island
0:00 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: So, in addition to the dangers faced by where someone works, sometimes, where you live can also make the impacts of extreme heat worse, like living in certain parts of a city.
0:10 · That’s because of a phenomenon called the urban heat island effect. A recent report by the research group Climate Central showed that more than 40 million Americans live in these hot spots.
0:22 · So, what does this mean for those residents? And is there any way that they can get relief?
0:26 · For that we turn to Michael Mendez. He’s assistant professor of environmental planning and policy at the University of California, Irvine, Professor Mendez, very good to have you on the “NewsHour.”
0:38 · Can you help us understand, what is the urban heat island effect?
0:43 · MICHAEL MENDEZ, University of California, Irvine: Thank you for having me here today.
0:45 · It’s a pleasure to be here to talk to you about this important issue that is affecting many people across the nation of – 40 percent of residents in the United States are under a heat advisory.
0:56 · And many of them are living in urban areas that don’t have adequate infrastructure, let alone environmental amenities like trees and other forms of greenery that provide shade. So, an urban heat island effect is where you have a lot of urbanized and suburban areas that are paved over with asphalt, concrete, other – other types of building materials that absorb the heat.
1:20 · And with little vegetation and trees for shade, and other types of cooling material, these areas and communities could be anywhere between five and even 20 degrees hotter than other neighborhoods that have more green space for shade.
1:36 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: That is such a shocking difference in temperature, simply that can be driven by how a certain area is built and what the structures are made of?
1:45 · MICHAEL MENDEZ: Most definitely.
1:47 · I think many of us that are millennials, Gen Z or even older remember being on the hot turf, asphalt, in schools and see the steam actually permeate out of these hard heat-trapping surfaces. So imagine not just a schoolyard, which is just, unfortunately, an urban heat island, but an entire neighborhood, and how that can really change how you experience an extreme heat event, and let alone if you’re living in an older home that’s 100 years old.
2:19 · You may experience it even more inside of a home that isn’t climate-resilient, can have even hotter temperatures than the ambient air outside.
2:29 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: I cited this study that says that millions of Americans are living in – under these types of conditions that you’re describing.
###### Can you explain why certain people are more vulnerable
2:36 · But I also understand that it is not equally spread across demographics, that certain people are more vulnerable than others. Can you explain?
2:46 · MICHAEL MENDEZ: Yes, this phenomenon of the heat waves, extreme climate events have disparate impact. It does not affect everyone equally.
2:55 · And this is because a heat wave is a natural phenomenon – phenomenon in some part, but there’s been political choices have been – have been made over the decades, if not centuries, that have withheld vital resources and infrastructure from communities, primarily low income-communities of color, African American and Latino communities.
3:16 · So it’s no surprise, when a heat wave or other type of extreme weather event strikes these communities, they’re the least prepared and often suffered the most impacts, because they don’t have – they have crumbling infrastructure, and they don’t have the amenities that will protect them and make them more resilient from our changing and extreme climate.
###### What are the possible solutions
3:36 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: You have been describing or hinting at a few of what seem like the possible solutions here.
3:42 · Walk me through those things. If I were a city planner, what are the things that I can do to try to reduce the urban heat island effect?
3:50 · MICHAEL MENDEZ: First is, a lot of these campaigns, we hear about the million-dollar campaigns that a lot of urban mayors are trying to institute in their cities, planting trees.
4:02 · Climate-resilient trees that are resilient to drought as well can help cool down are cities, cool roofs, rooftops, making sure that we’re able to reflect some of the sun’s rays and ensure that our houses that are cooler, having more climate weatherization in our homes, and, of course, having more cool pavements, moving away from that black asphalt and concrete that is absorbing and retaining the heat.
4:32 · And other types of green spaces, watershed management practices are multibenefit.
4:38 · They can clean our water, recharge our groundwater, but also provide green space and recreational spaces. So, we need to look for multibenefit policies and projects that attack multiple problems that climate change is causing our urban areas and throughout our nation.
###### What communities are doing
4:56 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: I mean, some of these things you’re talking about seem quite quick. I mean, you can paint a roof a different color quite quickly. Trees, we know, take longer to grow.
5:04 · Are there communities that you would point to in the country that are doing some of these things, that are taking this initiative head on and trying to do these things?
5:15 · MICHAEL MENDEZ: Well, in my in my hometown of Los Angeles, it’s often named – thought of as very suburban, very asphalt, concrete-driven area.
5:24 · But they’re really leading the way in terms of creating an Office of Climate Emergency and Resilience, where they have hired a full-time person and staff to understand these issues and try to target resources to the most immediate communities, those that are lacking these trees and green spaces.
5:44 · So, places like Los Angeles, Portland, of course, Seattle have been at the forefront of enacting climate action, but also having a strong equity and justice lens.
5:58 · WILLIAM BRANGHAM: All right, Professor Michael Mendez, U.C. Irvine, thank you so much for being here.
6:02 · MICHAEL MENDEZ: Thank you for having me.
#### Mexico’s Caribbean beaches overwhelmed by record sargassum influx I Inside Latin America
Al Jazeera English, 09 05, 2026
##### Video (10:02)
##### Description
The Caribbean is facing a growing sargassum seaweed crisis, with huge quantities washing onto beaches across the region and threatening tourism, ecosystems and public health.
Mexico’s Riviera Maya has been particularly affected, with the influx reaching thousands of tonnes of seaweed each day.
As the algae decomposes on beaches, it can release toxic gases, raising health concerns for residents and tourists. Scientists also warn that the accumulation of sargassum can damage seagrass ecosystems and affect beach stability.
The crisis is putting pressure on Mexico’s tourism industry, while the Mexican Navy has stepped up efforts to collect the seaweed before it reaches the shore.
But some businesses are looking at sargassum differently.
Companies including Carbonwave and Sargablock are developing ways to transform the invasive algae into products such as bio-fertilizers, footwear and construction materials.
Could an environmental crisis become a new source of sustainable materials?

0:04 · [music] Welcome to Inside Latin America and to Mexico’s Riviera Maya. For the last decade, growing amounts of algae called sargassum have been washing up on beaches across the Caribbean, transforming once pristine coastlines and threatening the communities that depend on them.
0:25 · From the beaches of Puerto Rico to the fishing villages of the Dominican Republic, this phenomenon is taking a toll. And 2026 brought record quantities with more than 12 million tons detected across the region. But where is it all coming from and why now?
0:42 · This is the Sargasso Sea. Scientists say around 2010, changing currents began moving seaweed from here into warmer waters, forming the Great Atlantic Sargasso Belt, a vast band of floating algae stretching from West Africa across the Atlantic. Then, nutrient pollution, including fertilizer runoff carried by rivers, helped fuel its explosive growth.
1:06 · Experts agree the regional response still doesn’t match the scale of the problem. But with so much sargassum arriving, governments are being forced to act while private citizens are looking to upcycle this seaweed in an effort to turn this new reality into an opportunity.

4:08 · They can put small boats which collect the sargassum. They can try to replenish the beach which is eroded.
4:15 · You just mentioned erosion. Can you tell me a little bit about how sargassum is having an effect on what’s already a problem?
4:23 · Sargassum just being there causes erosion. Then sometimes bad practices. So, they remove the sargassum from the beach, but they take a lot of sand with it, which is another problem. And the other problem the major, I think, which I think was one of the major problem because it’s my area of research, it kills the sea grasses in the nearshore. And sea grasses are very important to maintain stable beaches.
4:49 · Do you think of sargassum as a plague?
4:51 · It’s a marvelous seaweed. I I’m still amazed by it. It’s a a seaweed which spends its whole life floating in the ocean. It forms these enormous rafts in the open sea. And the open sea is a desert, basically, for a lot of organisms. And then in the middle of the desert, you have this really biodiverse a raft with small critters, with a lot of bacteria, with epibionts which which grow on the sargassum. It’s beautiful.
5:23 · But having these huge masses of biomass decaying, getting on the beach, and then getting into the sea, it it causes a enormous disequilibrium of of whole life here in these systems.
5:36 · We’ve spent the last few days looking at all the efforts on various different scales of trying to get rid of the sargassum, take it out of the sea, uh clean it from the beaches. It feels like a titanic effort.
5:49 · I think at least the people who make the decisions I I think they still don’t understand the scale they have to work at. But we should do 100 times more or 1,000 times more. So, it should be so much more and I think this should be a really, uh, coherent strategy of managing the sargassum.
6:09 · Are you concerned?
6:10 · Well, I think it’s very difficult to be positive in the in the scenario, isn’t that?
6:16 · Because, as I say, most likely, um, this is not going to stop. This ecosystem is already damaged, uh, beyond the level I won’t ever see it recovering.
6:29 · So, I think we we have to be realistic in this this scenario and I think it it’s very difficult to be realistic, of course, because it it doesn’t look very nice. But, either really scale up a lot and invert a lot and and I really try to diminish the quantity of sargassum getting to the shore or adapt.
6:50 · But what does adapting look like? For a series of entrepreneurs in this region, the answer lies in turning this sargassum into products that can have life beyond the sea. Today’s sargassum harvest in Puerto Morelos, on Mexico’s Caribbean coast, ready to be processed and turned into bio-fertilizer. It’s a time-sensitive operation. Tons of seaweed must be weighed, cleaned, and pressed before it starts to decompose.
7:21 · Carbonwave is a company specializing in upcycling sargassum, filtering it in multiple steps to extract nutrients that boost crop yields and reduce the need for chemical fertilizer. After years of development, its creators say they have finally secured export agreements with the US, Colombia, and Ecuador.
7:40 · We already prove that sargassum can be turned into something that is reliable financially that can create incomes, right? That can create economy along the communities here.
7:54 · In fact, the process sargassum that Carbonwave doesn’t use for agricultural products ends up on people’s feet. Sargasso Sandals mixes sargassum powder with EVA foam, the same material Nike and Adidas use in sneakers to make its soles. Each pair is between 10 and 15% sargassum.
8:14 · This is the fiber that I need.
8:16 · And not far from Carbonwave’s plant, Omar Vasquez is using sargassum to make building blocks.
8:22 · Sargablock is an artisanal process. Each block contains 40% sargassum. The rest is other organic materials. They can make up to 6,500 blocks per day [music] using some 40 tons of sargassum. Sargablock says they’ve already donated 21 small homes made of this material to poor families in this community. And they say interest in this product is growing, certainly locally. This truck is going to a local business around here that’s just starting a construction project.
8:54 · But Omar dreams of taking this product far beyond Mexico’s coasts.
8:59 · And I went to Africa, I went to Ghana, and the problem that they have with sargassum it’s major. Nobody talks about those countries because they don’t have the money that we have in Mexico because tourism they don’t go there as here. So, where I see myself bringing Sargablock all over the world and donating houses to all over the people.
9:17 · In Mexico alone, at least 200 projects are now looking for ways to turn a growing environmental problem into an economic opportunity. And while none of these solutions can single-handedly absorb the millions of tons of sargassum washing ashore each year, they are showing that this unwanted seaweed may have value beyond the beach.
9:40 · Governments and citizens alike are rushing to adapt the best they can to a phenomenon that is no longer just a seasonal nuisance, but rather an increasingly permanent fixture of coastal communities along the Caribbean. Thanks for watching Inside Latin America. Now back to Doha with more news.
9:58 · Still ahead on Al Jazeera, from buried underpants. Yes, you heard that

#### Omaha Explained Redlining
Cameron Logsdon, 10 09, 2020
##### Video (11:00)
##### Description
This (long-ass) video explores the history of redlining in Omaha, NE, the effects of which have lead to Omaha being one of the most segregated cities in the United States. The long-lasting impact of racist redlining practices in Omaha parallel many cities across the country. #### Superblocks How Barcelona is taking city streets back from cars
Vox, 09 27, 2016
##### Video (5:30)
##### Description
Modern cities are designed for cars. But the city of Barcelona is testing out an urban design trick that can give cities back to pedestrians.
Help us make more ambitious videos by joining the Vox Video Lab. It gets you exclusive perks, like livestream Q&As with all the Vox creators, a badge that levels up over time, and video extras bringing you closer to our work! Learn more at http://bit.ly/video-lab
Read more: http://www.vox.com/2016/8/4/12342806/barcelona-superblocks
Thumbnail image from http://shutterstock.com
Subscribe to our channel! http://goo.gl/0bsAjO
Vox.com is a news website that helps you cut through the noise and understand what’s really driving the events in the headlines. Check out http://www.vox.com to get up to speed on everything from Kurdistan to the Kim Kardashian app.
Check out our full video catalog: http://goo.gl/IZONyE
Follow Vox on Twitter: http://goo.gl/XFrZ5H
Or on Facebook: http://goo.gl/U2g06o
##### Transcript
0:01 · If you imagine a typical American city street, and you take away the space that’s dedicated to cars, you aren’t left with very much.
0:09 · There are some narrow walkways on the side, and some bridges in between them, but not much else.
0:13 · Cars dominate cities.
0:16 · Spend some time walking around most cities and you’ll find yourself pushed to narrow sidewalks, waiting for crosswalk lights.
0:20 · You’ll find cyclists navigating really narrow strips of space.
0:23 · Americans are used to cars the way that fish are used to water.
0:28 · That’s so ubiquitous in the U.S. that I think for most people, it just never occurred to them that it could be otherwise.
0:35 · But what if there were a way to change that?
0:36 · To give space back to pedestrians and bicyclists, and to make cities more friendly to life outside of a car?
0:43 · It turns out Barcelona might have a solution.
0:48 · In 2014, the city was faced with serious air pollution problems.
0:51 · Barcelona and its 35 surrounding municipalities consistently failed to meet the EU’s air quality targets.
0:57 · Studies were showing that air pollution in the region causes 3,500 premature deaths every year.
1:03 · Traffic in the city also causes severe noise pollution.
1:05 · So the city developed an extensive Urban Mobility Plan with the hope of reducing traffic by 21 percent.
1:11 · The coolest part of the plan were these things: They call them “superilles”.
1:17 · Superilles?
1:18 · “Si, superilles.”
1:19 · That translates to “superblocks”.
1:21 · It’s this urban design concept intended to minimize the presence of cars in city centers.
1:26 · The word “superblock” has been used before to describe huge city blocks without any passageways for cars.
1:31 · But that’s not what’s happening here.
1:33 · So here’s how Barcelona’s plan works.
1:35 · You take nine square city blocks and close off the inside to through traffic.
1:39 · So buses, big freight trucks — or any vehicles that are trying to get from one part of town to the next — have to drive around the perimeter.
1:47 · Inside the superblock, the speed limit is kept to 10 kilometers per hour — that translates to just over 6 miles per hour — and curbside parking is replaced by underground parking.
1:55 · That means you wind up with street space for markets, outdoor games, and events.
2:00 · Within this nine square block perimeter you’re gonna have kind of a pleasant streetscape where people can walk around and mingle and do things without this kind of constant fear of cars around.
2:13 · The concept is going to be tested out in five neighborhoods, but the city has identified 120 possible intersections throughout the region where it could be implemented.
2:22 · So how do we know what the results of this kind of plan would look like?
2:24 · Well, northwest of Barcelona is a city called Vitoria-Gasteiz, which has implemented superblock designs since 2008.
2:30 · In the main superblock at the city center, pedestrian space increased from 45 percent of the total surface area to 74 percent.
2:37 · With so much less traffic, noise levels dropped from 66.5 dBA to 61 dBA.
2:43 · Most impressive of all, there was a 42% reduction in nitrogen oxide emissions and a 38% reduction in particle pollution in the area.
2:51 · On top of that, business is up.
2:52 · What you consistently see when people change their streetscapes to prioritize human beings over cars is you don’t see any decline in economic activity, you see the opposite.
3:03 · You get more people walking and cycling around more slowly, stopping more often patronizing businesses more, and that … center of social activity will tend to build on itself.
3:15 · So here’s the question: could something like this work in an American city?
3:20 · Barcelona has some unique advantages getting started on this plan, in that a lot of it was built before cars, and a lot of it was built on a simple grid.
3:32 · The district of Eixample — where the superblock plan is based — was designed in 1859 in this repetitive grid structure by this guy, Ildefons Cerdá.
3:41 · He basically invented the word for (and the study of) “urbanization” when he laid out this grid plan for Barcelona that evenly distributed resources like schools and hospitals.
3:51 · But superblock designers insist that cities don’t need a simple grid structure to implement this kind of plan.
3:56 · It can work anywhere.
3:58 · Now, cities in the US have have attempted some car-minimizing projects like this.
4:01 · The problem is, they’re usually done in wealthier areas with lots of existing businesses.
4:07 · Zoning policies often require separation of residential and commercial areas — but an ideal walkable area would be a mix of the two.
4:14 · On top of that, zoning minimums on parking availability encourage the presence of cars and parking lots, and minimums on street width make for wide, unwalkable streets.
4:24 · Because of that, walkable districts are basically isolated luxury items in the US.
4:29 · What makes the Barcelona plan different is that they aren’t setting aside one fancy neighborhood or town square to make pedestrian-friendly — instead, by proposing superblocks throughout the entire city, they’ve declared car-free spaces a right for everybody, no matter what part of town they’re in.
4:44 · Maybe — this might be overly optimistic — but I think it has sunk in in the U. S. that the model whereby every city resident comes with a car — and drives a car everywhere – is just inherently limited.
4:57 · It limits the growth of your city, it limits the health of your city and the growth of your city.
5:01 · So one way or another we have to find ways of having a lot of people live close to one another without all of them having cars.
5:11 · You know, being able to get around and work and play in live and have enjoyable lives without cars.

In the small town of Estrada, Michigan a group of citizens has been fighting to hold the air force accountable for contaminating their waterways.
0:34 · In the first of a two part series, special correspondent Megan Thompson brings us their story.
0:39 · Tony and his family have been coming to their vacation home on a van in lake for decades, a relaxing getaway in northern Michigan but in 2016 he received a troubling letter from the estate.
1:02 · From the department of health and human services saying, don’t drink your water.
1:04 · One year later they began to notice something strange on the lake.
1:06 · It was December and my wife woke up and said I think it snowed last night. There was foam piled up all along the shoreline. You could see it for miles.
1:18 · He learned his tapwater, the lake, and the bright, white foam were all contaminated with toxic man-made chemicals.
1:30 · It is sensitive to a different place.
1:32 · It’s shocking.
1:34 · The base is across the lake. You can see the buildings.
1:36 · It turned out the chemicals were coming from the old air force base.
1:41 · It was a training area.
1:44 · Mark Henry is a retired environmental engineer for the state of Michigan that worked at the base after it closed in 1993.
1:50 · There used to be a big concree ball out here with simulated aircraft on it and they would bring the fire trucks in and let people practice.
###### What is PFAS
2:03 · Practice putting out jet fuel fire is dowsing the burning planes with a special military foam used in the military since the 1970’s made from a chemical called p-fas. Courtney Carrigan is an environmental epidemiologist at Michigan state university who studies human exposure to chemicals.
2:22 · Characterized by a chain of carbons with fluorine bond and a functional group. One and likes water and the other does not, it repels it.
2:31 · The useful properties mean they are contained in all kinds of common products like nonstick pans, stain resistant carpet, and food packaging. There are about 15,000 types of p-fas and increasing the research shows they can cause health problems from liver damage to heart disease and cancer.
2:49 · They are often called forever chemicals because they are so persistent in the environment. They do not break down naturally. They move easily into water and groundwater and end up in fish and drinking water.
###### The scale of the problem
3:04 · Today about 50% of the nation’s drinking water supply has p-fas in it and nearly every American has some level of p-fas in their blood. The military is one of the biggest p-fas polluters in the U.S. But the problem is only now becoming known. In 2000 a colleague of mark Henry’s made the discovery and it sounded the alarm. Today the Pentagon confirms or suspects pfas concomitant – contamination at almost 700 other sites.
3:40 · There is almost no place you can dig a hole on the base and not find pfas in the groundwater even to this day 50 years after the release.
3:51 · The groundwater does not stay on the base.
3:55 · These plumes are flowing from the former base.
3:57 · Seeping for years into nearby streams, Lakes, and the tapwater of some homeowners like Tony.
4:03 · Right there.
4:04 · A politically connected attorney, he was so alarmed he teamed up to launch “Need our water” and call attention to the environmental Carla – calamity. They gave pbs a tour of the areas most affected by the pfas.
###### The plume
4:30 · This is a ymca camp owned by Detroit.
4:33 · The water is coming off the base from the storm sewers flowing uninterrupted into the saddle river.
4:39 · Tests found pfas levels more than 50 times higher than allowed by Michigan law. Today we visited dozens were swimming and boating nearby. About one mile away is a wildlife area called Clarks marsh where birds have been found with some of the highest levels of pfas contamination ever documented. There are five health advisory for the area warning to not eat any fish you catch or animals you hunt.
5:08 · This lake is highly contaminated on the surface. When the wind agitates the surface of the water the foam can build up.
5:18 · Tests by the state foundpfas levels in the phone as – in the foam as high as 200,000 parts per trillion. The EPA standards for drinking water allow for four parts per trillion. This lake and the other contaminated streams drain into lake Huron, a source of drinking water for 10 million people in the U.S. And Canada. Contaminated foam has been found on its shores too.
5:45 · We can’t afford to allow this land, this natural resource to be poisonous for years and years and years to come. It has to stop.
5:58 · They spent the last seven years lobbying congress and the Pentagon to clean up the mess. > They can stop the flow of all the plumes of the property now.
###### Air Force delays
6:12 · Fort Smith is one of the first places and in a country where that pfas of the pollution is treated. At air force recently announced delays in cleaning the rest. Some systems won’t be operating for another four or five years.
6:32 · It is a slow motion.
6:35 · It’s molasses on a cold winter day.

6:47 · We feel abandoned.
6:48 · It is like you are >> Cooking the books. At a meeting in August board members invented frustration about the delays and said the cleanup was mismanaged accusing the military of not conducting enough testing before it started building the latest treatment system this summer.
7:05 · The air force did an extremely minimal amount of work to demonstrate the wells that were planning on putting in are actually in the right place.
7:16 · They are only treating 25% of the known problem. They have no idea, really, what exists beyond.

#### Why It’s Usually Hotter In A City Let’s Talk NPR
NPR, 08 14, 2018
##### Video (2:29)
##### Description
The way cities are designed and inhabited can make actually make them hotter. So what’s the solution? We explore a few ideas for beating the urban heat.
Subscribe to NPR - http://bit.ly/NPRsubscribe
• NPR explores how to cope with a warming world: https://n.pr/2AdziQ3
• Phoenix tries to reverse its ‘silent storm’ of heat deaths. https://n.pr/2zmQ6nb

Transcript

0:04 · There’s a reason that summers in the city are so hot, and it’s probably easiest to explain why with thermal vision.

0:11 · It shows the heat all around me and also the coldness of this ice cream cone.

0:16 · Cities are often hotter than their suburbs due to a phenomenon known as “the heat island effect.”

The Heat Island Effect

0:24 · All of that asphalt, concrete, dark rooftops and tall buildings— they absorb and store heat.

0:31 · And there are a lot of people using a lot of energy— driving cars, riding subways, running the AC.

0:38 · All of these materials and activities either create or retain heat.

0:44 · And all that stored heat causes steeper nighttime highs … when people would otherwise have a chance to cool down.

0:52 · The way all these heat-storing elements are laid out matters quite a bit.

0:57 · In cities laid out in grids, like Phoenix, Chicago, or Washington, D.C., the buildings are closer together, like closely packed coals in a fire, keeping heat in.

1:07 · More space between buildings creates more circulation.

1:11 · Another way to combat this heat island effect is through green space.

1:15 · Parks, trees, plants— they don’t absorb as much heat … they actually send water back in the atmosphere.

1:21 · Between all those hot buildings, that cools things off.

1:25 · Another solution is color.

Color Light

1:27 · Light colors reflect sunlight back into the atmosphere instead of storing it as heat.

1:31 · It’s easiest to see in crosswalks, and it’s true for the color of buildings and other urban surfaces, too.

1:39 · And one long-game way to combat the heat island effect is to avoid using air conditioning when you don’t need it.

Avoid Using Air Conditioning

1:45 · AC uses a ton of electricity and creates a feedback loop.

1:49 · When we get that electricity from coal or natural gas, that puts more carbon dioxide into the atmosphere … which warms the planet … making cities even hotter.

2:00 · But this isn’t just about comfort.

2:02 · Today, heat waves kill more people than any other extreme weather event.

2:07 · More than tornadoes, hurricanes and even floods.

2:11 · City populations keep growing, and those cities are only getting hotter.

2:17 · This is Let’s Talk, NPR’s news explainer show.

2:20 · Make sure to subscribe to our YouTube channel and check out other shows.

2:25 · I’m Christopher Joyce, and this is NPR.

20:32 · To answer that question, we have to understand another factor that makes these fires so destructive.

20:46 · Once the homes start to burn at scale, you’ve already lost fighting a structural fire, a first alarm, which is how firefighting resources are apportioned is at least 15 firefighters for one house, four engines, a truck and a BC is a general response. If one home is burning, any fire department anywhere can suppress that. At 10 homes, we now need 150 firefighters.

21:18 · If we’re going to effectively go in and no kidding, put that fire out, we need 150 firefighters and we need them right now because fire, the, the growth curve is exponential.

21:26 · Once the fire reaches homes, it changes into something else entirely.

21:31 · Once you have urban conflagration, structure to structure fire transmission, then all of the things we are talking about from a wild land setting are no longer effective. ’cause we are in a fundamentally different fire.

21:43 · The building itself, the structure can sustain a longer ignition compared to a tree. A tree can burn for, I dunno, a minute or two, a structure can burn for an hour.

21:53 · It’s the contents of those homes and businesses because that becomes fuel. And now we’re producing very large long burning embers that can fly with the wind to ignite.

22:05 · When we have dense neighborhoods, we have more cars, we have more fences, we have more sheds.

22:09 · Now we have two factors that combine to make fires unstoppable. Ember cast and structure to structure ignition. And under these conditions, the wildland firefighting playbook goes out the window. Air ops is grounded and embers jump the fire lines. So emergency response shifts from firefighting to an all-out race to save lives. And that’s exactly what happened in la.

22:37 · It was a wildland fire for probably about four to five minutes. So this fire burned straight, downhill into structures and then started burning structure to structure. The priority was rescue. So we’re gonna take care of life first and then save property as we can. The conditions were so bad that I couldn’t see the end of my hood. I had two hands come across my hood, put on my brakes.

23:01 · It was a a a, a middle aged lady and she says my dad is trapped in the, in the house. He can’t get out. So we immediately ran up to the house, grabbed him, got him out to my vehicle, and our fire personnel and our law enforcement personnel went on calls for service like that all through the night While assisting with evacuations. in Pacific Palisades was the first priority for Keegan and the community fire brigade.

23:35 · They also spent time doing last minute preparations on homes, clearing flammable items away from structures and extinguishing spot fires before they could ignite homes. But this was a decision that Keegan still grapples with.

23:50 · Somebody died on PCH, somebody died up Las Flores, somebody died in Big Rock.

23:56 · Maybe. How do you, how do you measure the importance of structures versus lives?
